Global in Time Vortex Configurations for the 2 2 D Euler Equations
Juan Dávila, Manuel del Pino, Monica Musso, Shrish Parmeshwar

Abstract
We consider the problem of finding a solution to the incompressible Euler equations ω t + v ⋅ ∇ ω = 0 ​ in ​ ℝ 2 × ( 0 , ∞ ) , v ⁡ ( x , t ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℝ 2 ( y − x ) ⟂ | y − x | 2 ​ ω ​ ( y , t ) ​ 𝑑 y \omega_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\omega=0\ \hbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,\infty),\quad v(x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{(y-x)^{\perp}}{|y-x|^{2}}\omega(y,t)\,dy\ that is close to a superposition of traveling vortices as t → ∞ t\to\infty . We employ a constructive approach by gluing classical traveling waves: two vortex-antivortex pairs traveling at main order with constant speed in opposite directions. More precisely, we find an initial condition that leads to a 4-vortex solution of the form ω ⁡ ( x , t ) = ω 0 ​ ( x − c ​ t ​ e ) − ω 0 ​ ( x + c ​ t ​ e ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ as ​ t → ∞ \omega(x,t)=\omega_{0}(x-ct\,e)-\omega_{0}(x+ct\,e)+o(1)\ \hbox{ as }t\to\infty where ω 0 ​ ( x ) = 1 ε 2 ​ W ​ ( x − q ε ) − 1 ε 2 ​ W ​ ( x + q ε ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ as ​ ε → 0 \omega_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}W\left(\frac{x-q}{\varepsilon}\right)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}W\left(\frac{x+q}{\varepsilon}\right)+o(1)\ \hbox{ as }\varepsilon\to 0 and W ⁡ ( y ) W(y) is a certain fixed smooth profile, radially symmetric, positive in the unit disc zero outside.



1
Introduction

This paper deals with the long-time behavior of solutions
\omega(x,t)
of the Euler equations for an incompressible, inviscid fluid in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In vorticity-stream formulation these equations take the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} \omega_{t}+\nabla^{\perp}\Psi\cdot\nabla\omega&=0\ \ \ \ &&\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T),\\
\Psi=(-\Delta)^{-1}&\omega\ \ &&\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T),\\
\omega(\cdot,0)=&\mathring{\omega}&&\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

The stream function
\Psi
is determined as the inverse Laplacian applied to
\omega
, yielding:

\Psi(x,t)=(-\Delta)^{-1}\omega(x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\omega(y,t)\,dy\ \

and the fluid velocity field
v=\nabla^{\perp}\Psi=(\Psi_{x_{2}},-\Psi_{x_{1}})
follows the Biot-Savart Law:

v(x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{(y-x)^{\perp}}{|y-x|^{2}}\omega(y,t)\,dy\ .

The Cauchy problem (
1.1
) is well-posed in the weak sense in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as deduced from the classical theories by Wolibner
[
34
]
and Yudovich
[
21
]
. Solutions are regular if the initial condition
\mathring{\omega}
is so. See for instance Chapter 8 in Majda-Bertozzi’s book
[
22
]
.

We are interested in solutions of (
1.1
) exhibiting nontrivial dynamics as
t\to\infty
. Specifically, we are interested in solutions with vorticity
concentrated
around a finite number of points
\xi_{1}(t),\ldots,\xi_{N}(t)
, to fix ideas, in the form

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{m_{j}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Big[W\left(\frac{x-\xi_{j}(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)+o(1)\Big],

(1.2)

where
W(y)
is a fixed radially symmetric profile with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W(y)dy=1
,
o(1)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
and
m_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
are the circulations of the vortices. We have that

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\rightharpoonup\omega_{S}(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}{m_{j}}\delta_{0}({x-\xi_{j}(t)})\quad\hbox{as }\varepsilon\to 0,

(1.3)

where
\delta_{0}(y)
designates a Dirac mass at
0
.
Formally the trajectories
\xi_{j}(t)
satisfy the Kirchhoff-Routh vortex system

\dot{\xi}_{j}(t)=-\sum_{\ell\not=j}\frac{m_{\ell}}{2\pi}\frac{(\xi_{j}(t)-\xi_{\ell}(t))^{\perp}}{|\xi_{j}(t)-\xi_{\ell}(t)|^{2}},\quad j=1,\ldots,N.

(1.4)

In
[
10
]
the following was proven:
Given a finite time
T>0
and a solution
\left(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{N}\right)
to System (
1.4
) with no collisions in
[0,T]
, namely with

\inf_{t\in[0,T],\,i\neq j}|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)|>0,

there exists a solution of (
1.1
) with the precise form (
1.2
) and the specific choice

W(y)=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}
.

In
[
24
]
it was previously shown that an initial condition supported on
\varepsilon
-disks concentrated around the points
\xi_{j}(0)
as bumps with masses
m_{j}
lead to a solution which as
\varepsilon\to 0
satisfies the distributional convergence (
1.3
) but without the asymptotic description (
1.2
) near the vortices.

The finiteness in time
T>0
is essential to get any form of uniform estimates in
[0,T]
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the results in
[
24
]
and
[
10
]
. It is not clear that the solution as time evolves, for fixed
\varepsilon
will keep the form (
1.2
) while trajectories of system (
1.4
) generically do not collide, see Chapter 4 in
[
25
]
.

This paper deals with the construction of solutions to
the Euler equations (
1.1
) that keep
the form (
1.2
)
at all times
.

The problem of understanding long-time patterns for solutions of (
1.1
) still
carries many mysteries, while specific conjectures have been raised. See for instance
[
13
]
and its references.

In the analysis of long-time dynamics, a fundamental class is that of
steady solutions
, namely solutions whose shape does not depend on time. Examples include radially symmetric decaying functions
W(y)
, which correspond to stationary solutions. Other examples are traveling and rotating waves. See for instance
[
2
,
16
,
31
,
7
]
and references therein. The problem of finding such solutions is typically reduced to solving semilinear elliptic equations.

The problem of finding time-dependent configurations that, at their core, closely resemble the superposition of steady solutions, perhaps with the profile (
1.2
), is indeed a challenging one. This challenge arises due to the emergence of coupling error terms in the main order, and our understanding of the long-term behavior of perturbations to steady solutions still remains limited.

We will consider a geometrically simple configuration that however contains many of the difficulties of more complex situations: that of two vortex pairs traveling at constant, opposite speeds along the
x_{1}
-axis.

Let us first describe a single vortex pair.
Let
e_{1}=(1,0),\ e_{2}=(0,1)
,
q_{\infty}>0
,
M>0
.
A point vortex pair solution to (
1.4
) is given by
\xi_{1}^{pair}(t)
,
\xi_{2}^{pair}(t)
with
m_{1}=M=-m_{2}
and

\displaystyle\xi_{1}^{pair}(t)=q_{\infty}e_{2}+c_{\infty}te_{1},\quad\xi_{2}^{pair}(t)=-q_{\infty}e_{2}+c_{\infty}te_{1},\quad c_{\infty}=\frac{M}{4\pi}.

Associated to this solution, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough, there exists an
\varepsilon
-concentrated traveling vortex pair solving (
1.1
) that can be described as

\omega(x,t)=U_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}}(x-c_{\varepsilon,\infty}te_{1}),

where
U_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}}=\varepsilon^{-2}f_{\varepsilon}(\Psi(x)-c_{\varepsilon,\infty}x_{2})
and, for an appropriate nonlinearity
f_{\varepsilon}
,
\Psi
solves the elliptic problem

\displaystyle-\Delta\Psi(x)=\varepsilon^{-2}f_{\varepsilon}(\Psi(x)-c_{\varepsilon,\infty}x_{2}).

(1.5)

in the upper half plane
x_{2}>0
,
and is even in
x_{1}
and odd in
x_{2}
.
The function
U_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}}
can be written as

U_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}}(x)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[\Gamma^{\gamma}_{+}\left(\frac{x-q_{\infty}e_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\Gamma^{\gamma}_{+}\left(\frac{x+q_{\infty}e_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)+\,\phi_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\right],\quad

where
w(x)
is an entire radial classical solution of the problem

\displaystyle\Delta\Gamma+\Gamma^{\gamma}_{+}=0\ \text{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\{\Gamma>0\}=B_{1}

(1.6)

and

\|\phi_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}}\|_{\infty}=O(\varepsilon^{2})
,
\mbox{supp}(\phi_{\varepsilon,q_{\infty}})\subseteq B_{R\varepsilon}(0,q_{\infty})\cup B_{R\varepsilon}(0,-q_{\infty})

for some fixed
R>0
.
The speed
c_{\varepsilon,\infty}
has the expansion

c_{\varepsilon,\infty}=\frac{M}{4\pi q_{\infty}}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

where

\displaystyle M\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{+}dx.

(1.7)

A solution of this type was first found in
[
31
]
via variational methods. In Theorem
2.1
below we perform a fixed point construction that provides the fine information needed in this paper. See
[
2
,
6
,
7
,
27
]
for related results and
[
4
,
27
,
33
]
for earlier traveling solutions.

There is a 4 point solution the Kirchhoff-Routh vortex system (
1.4
), that as
t\to\infty
behaves as two vortex pairs traveling in opposite directions, and is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} m_{1}&=-m_{2}=M,\quad\xi_{*{},1}(t)=\left(p_{*{}}(t),q_{*{}}(t)\right),&\xi_{*{},2}(t)&=\left(p_{*{}}(t),-q_{*{}}(t)\right),\\
m_{3}&=-m_{4}=M,\quad\xi_{*{},3}(t)=\left(-p_{*{}}(t),-q_{*{}}(t)\right),&\xi_{*{},4}(t)&=\left(-p_{*{}}(t),q_{*{}}(t)\right),\end{aligned}

(1.8)

where

\displaystyle p_{*{}}(t)=\frac{M}{4\pi q_{\infty}}t+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t}\Bigr),\quad q_{*{}}(t)=q_{\infty}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\Bigr),

and
M>0
,
q_{\infty}>0
. See Section
3
for details.

It is thus natural to ask whether an
initial vorticity consisting of four compactly supported
\varepsilon
-concentrated regular functions, identical except for alternating
sign, located symmetrically in the four quadrants, generates a time evolved vorticity that remains localised around the four points
\xi_{*{},j}(t)
,
j=1,\ldots,4
, at all times
t
.
We state our result in the following form

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\gamma\geq 19
,
q_{\infty}>0
. Then for all
T_{0}>0
large enough such that
p_{*}
given in (
1.8
) satisfies
p_{*{}}(T_{0})\geq 10q_{\infty}
, and all
\varepsilon>0
small enough there is a constant
C>0
, such that there exists a solution to (
1.1
),
(\omega_{\varepsilon},\Psi_{\varepsilon})
, on the whole interval
[T_{0},\infty)
, with

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=U_{\varepsilon,q_{*}+q_{re}}\left(x-\left(p_{*}+p_{re}\right)e_{1}\right)-U_{\varepsilon,q_{*}+q_{re}}\left(x+\left(p_{*}+p_{re}\right)e_{1}\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,t),

(1.9)

where the point
\left(p_{*}(t),q_{*}(t)\right)
is defined in (
1.8
), and
p_{re}(t)
and
q_{re}(t)
are functions
[T_{0},\infty):\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and satisfy, for all
t\in\left[T_{0},\infty\right)
,

\displaystyle\left|t^{-1}p_{re}\left(t\right)\right|+\left|\dot{p}_{re}\left(t\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle\left|t^{2}q_{re}\left(t\right)\right|+\left|t^{3}\dot{q}_{re}\left(t\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

The function
\phi_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.9
) is continuous, odd in both variables
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, and satisfies

|\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,t)|\leq C\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\right)^{1-\sigma},\quad\|\phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}\left(Q\right)}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{t^{2-\sigma}},\quad\text{for all }t>T_{0},

for any
\sigma>0
small.
If restricted to the first quadrant
Q=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\,:\,x_{1}>0,x_{2}>0\}
it satisfies

{\mbox{supp}}\,\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\subseteq B_{C\varepsilon}(\left(p_{*}+p_{re},q_{*}+q_{re}\right)),\quad\text{for all }t>T_{0}.

The functions
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x,t+T_{0})
,
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t+T_{0})
,
t>0
form a solution of (
1.1
) in
[0,\infty)
and has the expected properties in the whole interval
[0,\infty)
. The condition
\gamma\geq 19
is a technical hypothesis required in the proof of some a priori estimates.

Our result gives a contribution to the understanding of the long-time behavior of solutions to (
1.1
), a problem that, as we remarked previously, remains largely open. We construct specific initial conditions that allow us to exhibit the long time behavior shown in Theorem
1.1
, but we do not claim that small perturbations of our initial data will preserve this structure. Physical experiments and numerical simulations suggest that most solutions evolve over infinite time towards simpler dynamics. Orbital stability for vortex pairs was proven in
[
5
]
. Stability for Lamb dipoles has been established in
[
1
]
. Regarding the long-time behavior of solutions to the
2
-d Euler equation, the following informal conjecture has been formulated in
[
32
]
,
[
30
]
: as
t\to\pm\infty
generic solutions experience loss of compactness.
Significant confirmations of this conjecture have emerged near specific steady states. Important stability results for shear flows have been found in
[
3
,
18
,
19
,
26
]
. Stability results for fast-decay, radial steady states have been found
[
3
,
18
,
8
]
. See also
[
13
]
for discrete symmetry configurations.

Our result is consistent with the confinement result in
[
17
]
and provides much finer qualitative information on the vorticity at all time
t
.
Vorticity confinement results regard bounding the expansion of the support for the vorticity, when initially this is compactly supported and in
L^{\infty}
.
Since the velocity is bounded, the radius of the support can grow at most linearly in time. When the initial vorticity has a definite sign, the radius of the support grows more slowly. We refer the reader to
[
23
,
17
,
29
]
and references therein.

Well-known non-smooth standard flows are vortex patches, a category our solution does not belong to. For such solutions there are several results on long time dynamics.
In
[
35
]
, a global solution to (
1.1
) consisting of vortex patches centered around an expanding self-similar solution to (
1.4
) with
N=3
was found. But
[
35
]
deals with patch solutions and one may wonder about the existence of more regular ones. Also, in
[
35
]
each patch may grow in size at a rate
t^{\frac{1}{4}^{+}}
, but they separate from each other at a rate
t^{\frac{1}{2}}
. This seems crucial in the analysis.
The existence of co-rotating and counter-rotating pair vortex patches is contained in
[
16
]
. Logarithmic spiraling solutions were recently constructed in
[
20
]
. For other results concerning regularity and long-term behavior of vortex patches, we refer to
[
12
]
and to the results they cite.

We discuss the scheme of the proof in the next section.

2
Scheme of the proof

The strategy we adopt to construct the solution predicted by Theorem
1.1
is to first produce a solution to the Euler equations (
1.1
) on a finite and arbitrarily large time interval
[T_{0},T]
, for
T\gg T_{0}
. This solution is built as a perturbation of the sum of two
\varepsilon
-concentrated vortex pairs traveling in opposite directions. The perturbation will be small and decay in time. To conclude our argument we apply the Ascoli-Arzelá Theorem for a sequence of times
(T_{n})_{n}
, with
T_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and show that the limit function gives the desired solution in the whole interval
[T_{0},\infty)
. We do this in Section
6
.

The main step in our proof is the construction of the solution to the Euler equations (
1.1
) on
[T_{0},T]
as described above. The starting point for this construction is the basic
\varepsilon
-concentrated traveling vortex pair of which we need a fine understanding. This precise information is contained in
[
11
]
and is summarized in Subsection
2.1
. The approximate solution is built upon the sum of two vortex pairs corrected by a relatively explicit function in order to produce a sufficiently small error. We describe this in Subsection
2.3
. Detailed proofs are contained in Section
4
. We then solve the problem on
[T_{0},T]
, as explained in Subsection
2.4
, with full details in Section
5
.

2.1
Traveling vortex pair

Recall (
1.5
), which tells us that to achieve a vortex pair with
\varepsilon
-concentrated vorticity, we want solve the semilinear elliptic PDE

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{-2}f_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}-cx_{2})=0,\quad\omega_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-2}f_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}-cx_{2})

(2.1)

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for a choice of power type non-linearity
f_{\varepsilon}
that we introduce next.

In Section
1
we introduced the function
\Gamma
solving (
1.6
), which we can write as

\Gamma(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nu(|x|)&\text{if }|x|\leq 1\\
\nu^{\prime}(1)\log{|x|}&\text{if }|x|>1\end{array}\right.,

where
\nu(|x|)
is the unique positive, radial, ground state solving

\Delta\nu+\nu^{\gamma}_{+}=0\ \text{on}\ B_{1}.

Recalling that

M=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{+}dx

(see (
1.7
)),
a direct computation gives
M=2\pi\left|\nu^{\prime}(1)\right|.

The quantity
\left|\nu^{\prime}(1)\right|
will be an important one in our construction, therefore we will call it the reduced mass, and label it by
m

\displaystyle m\coloneqq\frac{M}{2\pi}=\left|\nu^{\prime}(1)\right|.

(2.2)

For any
\varepsilon>0
, rescaling
\Gamma
by
\varepsilon
leaves it
C^{1}
across the boundary of the ball radius
\varepsilon
, as seen by the following computation:

\partial_{r}\left(\nu\left(\frac{|x|}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\biggr\rvert_{r=\varepsilon}=\frac{\nu^{\prime}(1)}{\varepsilon},\quad\partial_{r}\left(-m\log{\left(\frac{|x|}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right)\biggr\rvert_{r=\varepsilon}=-\frac{m}{\varepsilon},

where we note that matching occurs due to (
2.2
) and the fact that
\nu^{\prime}(1)<0
. Thus, the rescaled
\Gamma
solve the following family of semilinear elliptic equations

\displaystyle\Delta\Gamma\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)+\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)^{\gamma}_{+}=0,

(2.3)

which are solutions with concentrated vorticity in the sense of (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) for each
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Observe that for all
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)^{\gamma}_{+}dx=\int_{B_{1}}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{+}\left(z\right)\ dz=M.

(2.4)

We will build a travelling vortex pair solution using the rescaled
\Gamma
and our family of
f_{\varepsilon}
will be chosen using the power type nonlinearity in (
2.3
). Concretely, let
q_{\infty}>0
. Then our solution will have the form

\displaystyle\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}(x)+\psi_{\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right),\quad{\mbox{where}}\quad\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\Gamma\left(\frac{x-q_{\infty}e_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\Gamma\left(\frac{x+q_{\infty}e_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right),

(2.5)

with
f_{\varepsilon}
defined by

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)-cx_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(q_{\infty}e_{2}\right)}(x)

\displaystyle-\left(-\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)+cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(-q_{\infty}e_{2}\right)}(x).

(2.6)

Here
s>0
is a fixed constant, and the
\chi
are indicator functions. To find such a solution we will need to choose our
c
and
\Omega
as a function of
q_{\infty}
. The quantity
\Omega
is a parameter to adjust the mass of our vortex pair.
We demand that

\displaystyle-\Gamma\left(\frac{2q_{\infty}e_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-cq_{\infty}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega=0.

(2.7)

Recalling (
1.6
), this means

m\log{\left(\frac{2q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}\right)}-cq_{\infty}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega=0,

which is satisfied if and only if

\displaystyle\Omega=m+\frac{m\log{2q_{\infty}}-cq_{\infty}}{\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|}.

(2.8)

This choice guarantees that

M-\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}(x)-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(q_{\infty}e_{2}\right)}(x)\ dx=O(\varepsilon),\quad\hbox{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

If we suppose
q_{\infty}\in\left(Q_{0}^{-1},Q_{0}\right)
, some
Q_{0}>0
large and
c=c(q_{\infty},\varepsilon)
has the bound
c\leq mQ_{0}
for all
\varepsilon
small enough, then
the support of
f_{\varepsilon}(\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}(x)-cx_{2})
shrinks as
\varepsilon\to 0
: there exists
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough such that for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,

{\mbox{supp}}\,f_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}(x)-cx_{2}\right)\subseteq B_{\rho_{0}\varepsilon}(q_{\infty}e_{2})\cup B_{\rho_{0}\varepsilon}(-q_{\infty}e_{2})

for

\displaystyle\rho_{0}=2\left(1+\frac{sQ_{0}}{2}\right)e^{Q_{0}^{2}},

(2.9)

and

\displaystyle\left|\left(\Gamma(y-\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}e_{2})\right)^{\gamma}_{+}-\left(\Gamma\left(y-\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}e_{2}\right)-\Gamma\left(y+\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}e_{2}\right)-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{\frac{s}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}e_{2}\right)}\right|\leq C\varepsilon\chi_{B_{\rho_{0}}(\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}e_{2})}(y),

with an analogous statement holding around
-\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}e_{2}
via symmetry in
y_{2}
.

Our non-linearity
f_{\varepsilon}
in (
2.6
) will have the desired regularity due to the fact that our choice of cutoff represented by the indicator functions means that its support will actually be contained in
\chi_{B_{s^{\prime}}\left(q_{\infty}e_{2}\right)}(x)\cup\chi_{B_{s^{\prime}}\left(q_{\infty}e_{2}\right)}(x)
, some
s^{\prime}<s
, for small enough
\varepsilon
, meaning the nonlinearity is
C^{\lfloor\gamma\rfloor,\gamma-\left[\gamma\right]}
when
\gamma\notin\mathbb{Z}
and
C^{\gamma-1}
otherwise.
In fact we can think of
\psi_{\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}}
in (
2.5
) as a comparatively small error term. Note then, that to main order, both our solution and nonlinearity look like an odd extension of the solution and nonlinearity defined in (
2.3
). We will find a solution
\psi_{\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}}
to our problem with the same oddness in the
x_{2}
direction.

The existence and properties of our
\varepsilon
-concentrated traveling vortex pair are summarized in the following result. The existence of such a solution can be seen in
[
31
]
, and the finer properties of the solution claimed in the following theorem are shown in
[
11
]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\gamma>3
in (
1.6
) and let
q_{\infty}>0.
For all
\varepsilon>0
small, there exist constants
C>0
,
c
and a function
\psi_{\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}}(x)
even in
x_{1}
, odd in
x_{2}
such that
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
given by (
2.5
) with
\Omega
as in (
2.8
) solves (
2.1
), with
f_{\varepsilon}
given by (
2.6
). Set

q_{\infty}^{\prime}=\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}.

The speed
c
is given by

c=\frac{m}{2q_{\infty}}+g(q_{\infty}^{\prime})

(2.10)

and the following estimates hold true

\displaystyle\|\psi_{q_{\infty}^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{q_{\infty}^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\partial_{q_{\infty}^{\prime}}^{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

(2.11)

\displaystyle|g|

\displaystyle+|\partial_{q_{\infty}^{\prime}}g|+|\partial_{q_{\infty}^{\prime}}^{2}g|\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

(2.12)

Note that whilst the magnitude of the mass for both vortices in the travelling pair will be the same by symmetry, the pair itself will have total mass
0
due to the signs of each separate vortex.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that the support of
f_{\varepsilon}\left(\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)-cx_{2}\right)
, with
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
given by (
2.5
), and
\psi_{\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying bounds (
2.11
), is contained in

B_{\rho\varepsilon}(q_{\infty}e_{2})\cup B_{\rho\varepsilon}(-q_{\infty}e_{2}).

for some
\rho>\rho_{0}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
,
\rho_{0}
defined in (
2.9
).

2.2
Modified vortex trajectories

From Theorem
2.1
we learn that the speed of an
\varepsilon
-concentrated traveling vortex pair is the speed of an idealized traveling vortex pair
\frac{m}{2q_{\infty}}
modified by a correction
g
of size
\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle c=\frac{m}{2q_{\infty}}+g\left(q_{\infty}^{\prime}\right),\quad q_{\infty}^{\prime}=\frac{q_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}.

The presence of this additional term, denoted as
g
, has implications for our construction of the two-vortex pair configuration. The idealized system (
1.4
) for
N=4
will need to be adjusted to incorporate this error term, and the idealized point vortex solution (
1.8
) will require an additional term of magnitude
\varepsilon^{2}
.

Write the ODE system (
1.4
) with
N=4
for the points
\xi_{*{},j}
,
j=1,2,3,4
as in (
1.8
) as

\dot{p}_{*{}}=\mathcal{F}\left(q_{*{}},p_{*{}}\right),\quad\dot{q}_{*{}}=-\mathcal{F}\left(p_{*{}},q_{*{}}\right),

(2.13)

where

\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\left\{\left(0,0\right)\right\}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(x,y\right)\coloneqq\frac{m}{2}\left(\frac{1}{x}-\frac{x}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\right)=\frac{m}{2}\left(\frac{y^{2}}{x\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)}\right).

(2.14)

Here, and for the rest of the paper, we fix

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}q_{*{}}(t)=q_{\infty}\in\left(Q_{0}^{-1},Q_{0}\right),

(2.15)

where
Q_{0}
is some large positive constant.

Then the modified vortex trajectories

\xi_{0}(t)=\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)

obey the modified system

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\left(q_{0},p_{0}\right)+g\left(q_{0}^{\prime}\right),\quad\dot{q}_{0}=-\mathcal{F}\left(p_{0},q_{0}\right)

(2.16)

where
g
is the
\varepsilon^{2}
-correction to the speed of an idealized vortex pair, as described in Theorem
2.1
and (
2.10
).
In Section
3
, Theorem
3.1
, we construct a solution to the system (
2.16
) in the form

\xi_{0}(t)=\xi_{*{}}(t)+\overline{\xi}(t),\quad\overline{\xi}(t)=\left(\overline{p}(t),\overline{q}(t)\right),

(2.17)

where
\overline{\xi}
will be a small error term.
The functions
\bar{p}
and
\bar{q}
, defined on the interval
[T_{0},\infty)
and mapping to
\mathbb{R}
, satisfy the first and second estimates presented in (
2.34
), respectively.

2.3
Approximate solution on
[T_{0},T]

Our initial step in developing a two-vortex pair solution to the Euler equations (
1.1
) is to create a reasonably accurate first approximation within a finite time interval
\left[T_{0},T\right]
. We treat
T
as an arbitrarily large parameter, with the intention of eventually taking
T\to\infty
to construct our complete solution over the interval
\left[T_{0},\infty\right)
. The approximation as well as the solution are odd in both spacial variables
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.

We fix
T>0
, a large time satisfying

\displaystyle T\gg T_{0}.

(2.18)

To describe the approximate solution we first introduce

\displaystyle\xi(t)=(p(t),q(t)),

(2.19)

a time dependent vector field, moving in the first quadrant
x_{1}\geq 0
,
x_{2}\geq 0
. We assume it has the form

\displaystyle\xi(t)=\xi_{0}(t)+\hat{\xi}(t),\quad{\mbox{with}}\quad\hat{\xi}(t)=\xi_{1}(t)+\tilde{\xi}(t).

(2.20)

The first term

\xi_{0}(t)=\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)
is explicit and given by (
2.17
). The point
\xi_{1}(t)=\left(p_{1}(t),q_{1}(t)\right)

will also be determined in the process of the construction of
the approximation, and will be obtained as part of a solvability condition for an elliptic problem. It will satisfy

\displaystyle\left\|tp_{1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty)}+\left\|t^{2}\dot{p}_{1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty)}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\left\|t^{2}q_{1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty)}+\left\|t^{3}\dot{q}_{1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty)}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|,

for some constant
C>0
. The point

\tilde{\xi}(t)=\left(\tilde{p}(t),\tilde{q}(t)\right)
is a free parameter to adjust at the end of our construction to obtain a true solution on
[T_{0},T]
. For the
moment, we ask that it is a continuous function in
[T_{0},T]
for which
\dot{\tilde{\xi}}
exists and such that

\displaystyle\left\|t\tilde{p}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[T_{0},T\right]}+\left\|t^{2}\dot{\tilde{p}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[T_{0},T\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{4-\sigma},\ \ \tilde{p}\left(T\right)=0,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\left\|t^{2}\tilde{q}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[T_{0},T\right]}+\left\|t^{3}\dot{\tilde{q}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[T_{0},T\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{4-\sigma},\ \ \tilde{q}\left(T\right)=0,

for some
\sigma>0
arbitrarily small.

Having introduced the point
\xi(t)
in (
2.19
) with the form (
2.20
) and bounds (
2.21
)-(
2.22
), we let

c(t)
be dependent on
q(t)
as in (
2.10
) so that

\displaystyle c(t)=\frac{m}{2q(t)}+g\left(\frac{q(t)}{\varepsilon}\right),

(2.23)

and
\Omega(t)
as in (
2.8
) so that

\displaystyle\Omega(t)=m+\left(\frac{m\log{2q(t)}-c(t)q(t)}{\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|}\right).

(2.24)

We start our construction with a stream function
\Psi_{0}[\xi](x,t)
defined as the sum of the stream function of a vortex pair traveling to the right, denoted as
\Psi_{R}
, and one of a vortex pair traveling to the left, denoted by
\Psi_{L}
. We proceed in a similar way with the vorticity. We write

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}[\xi](x,t)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{R}(x,t)-\Psi_{L}(x,t),

(2.25)

\displaystyle\omega_{0}[\xi](x,t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}\left(U_{R}(x,t)-U_{L}(x,t)\right),

with

\displaystyle\Psi_{R}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}-qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\Gamma\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}+qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)+\psi_{\frac{q}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi_{L}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}-qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\Gamma\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}+qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)+\psi_{\frac{q}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right),

and

\displaystyle U_{R}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{R}(x)-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(qe_{2}\right)}\left(x-pe_{1}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(-\Psi_{R}(x)+cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(-qe_{2}\right)}\left(x-pe_{1}\right)

\displaystyle U_{L}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{L}(x)+cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(qe_{2}\right)}\left(x+pe_{1}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(-\Psi_{L}(x)-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(-qe_{2}\right)}\left(x+pe_{1}\right)

Here we recall the definition of

\Gamma
from (
1.6
),
\psi_{\frac{q}{\varepsilon}}
from (
2.5
) with the definition of
\xi
(
2.20
) in hand, and
c(t),\Omega(t)
related to
q(t)
by (
2.23
)–(
2.24
).

We introduce the Euler operators

\displaystyle E_{1}\left[\Psi,\omega\right]

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\omega(x,t)+\nabla^{\perp}\Psi(x,t)\cdot\nabla\omega(x,t),

(2.26)

\displaystyle E_{2}\left[\Psi,\omega\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta\Psi(x,t)+\omega(x,t).

The error of approximation for
\left(\Psi_{0}[\xi](x,t),\omega_{0}[\xi](x,t)\right)
is an odd function in both variables
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
such that, when restricted to the first quadrant, for some constant
C>0
it satisfies

|E_{1}\left[\Psi_{0}[\xi],\omega_{0}[\xi]\right]|\leq C\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{(1+t)^{2}},\quad{\mbox{supp}}\,\left(E_{1}\left[\Psi_{0}[\xi],\omega_{0}[\xi]\right]\right)\subseteq B_{\mathcal{K}\varepsilon}(p,q),

where
\mathcal{K}
is a fixed positive number independent of
\varepsilon
. By construction we have

-\Delta\Psi_{j}=\varepsilon^{-2}U_{j},\ \ \ j=L,R.

Hence

E_{2}\left[\Psi_{0}[\xi],\omega_{0}[\xi]\right]=0.

In Section
4
we prove there exists an improved approximation
(\Psi_{*}[\xi](x,t),\omega_{*}[\xi](x,t))

such that the new error
E_{1}\left[\Psi_{*},\omega_{*}\right]
is odd in
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, and when restricted to the first quadrant, for some constant
C>0
it satisfies

|E_{1}\left[\Psi_{*}[\xi],\omega_{*}[\xi]\right]|\leq C\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\sigma}}{(1+t)^{3}},\quad{\mbox{supp}}\,\left(E_{1}\left[\Psi_{*}[\xi],\omega_{*}[\xi]\right]\right)\subseteq B_{\mathcal{K}_{1}\varepsilon}(p,q).

Here
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is a fixed positive number independent of
\varepsilon
.
Besides

|E_{2}\left[\Psi_{*}[\xi],\omega_{*}[\xi]\right]|\leq C\frac{\varepsilon^{4-\sigma}}{(1+t)^{3}}.

In the above estimates,

\sigma
is a positive number, arbitrarily small.

A crucial step in the construction of the improved approximation is to recognize the very special structure of the principal parts of the linear terms in
E_{1}\left[\Psi_{0}[\xi]+\Psi,\omega_{0}[\xi]+\omega\right]
. This structure manifests on the right hand side of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the form of a linear elliptic problem, as

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Psi_{R}-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\left(t\right)\right)\cdot\nabla\left(\mathscr{L}\left(\psi\right)\right)=G,

where
\mathscr{L}
is the linearized operator around the vortex pair travelling to the right, for some right hand side
G
. By symmetry, there is a completely analogous structure on the left hand side of the plane. As it turns out, a key insight will be the fact that we can actually write
G
as

\displaystyle G=\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Psi_{R}-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\left(t\right)\right)\cdot\nabla\tilde{G}+\hat{G},

(2.27)

where
\tilde{G}
has desirable properties, and
\hat{G}
is small enough to deal with in the last step of the construction. These observations allow us to construct the first approximation by solving elliptic problems of the form

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\left(\psi\right)=\tilde{G}.

It should be noted that to discover the structure of the right hand side (
2.27
) required very precise knowledge on how the travelling wave steady states depend on the parameter
q(t)
.

This allows us to improve the initial approximation
(\Psi_{0}[\xi],\omega_{0}[\xi])
solving linear elliptic problems. This can be done provided the point
\xi_{1}
in the decomposition of
\xi
in (
2.20
) is properly adjusted and under the assumption that
\tilde{\xi}
satisfies (
2.22
). The approximate vorticity
\omega_{*}[\xi]
is found of the form

\omega_{*}=\omega_{0}+\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\phi_{R}-\phi_{L}\right),

(2.28)

so that
\phi_{R}
has the same support as
U_{R}
, is odd in
x_{2}
and satisfies the bound

\|\phi_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1+t)^{2}},

with an analogous statement for
\phi_{L}.

2.4
Construction of a solution

Next, let
K>0
be a constant. Define cutoffs by

\eta^{(R)}_{K}(x,t)\coloneqq\eta_{0}\left(\frac{\left|x-pe_{1}\right|}{K(1+t)}\right),\quad\eta^{(L)}_{K}(x,t)\coloneqq\eta_{0}\left(\frac{\left|x+pe_{1}\right|}{K(1+t)}\right)

where
\eta_{0}(r)
is a cutoff that is
1
on
r\leq 1
and
0
on
r\geq 2
. From Remark
2.2
we have

\supp U_{R}\subset B_{\varepsilon\rho}\left(p,q\right)\cup B_{\varepsilon\rho}\left(p,-q\right),

for some absolute constant
\rho>0
. This is even with time dependence due to the size of
\xi_{0}
we will construct in Section
3
, and the sizes of
\xi_{1}
and
\tilde{\xi}
from (
2.21
)–(
2.22
). We can choose
K
as

\displaystyle K=5q_{\infty}.

(2.29)

The choice of
K
in (
2.29
) ensures
\eta^{(j)}_{K}\equiv 1
on
\supp{U_{j}}
for
j=R,L
, and the choice of
T_{0}
, given in Theorem
1.1
, so that
p_{*}\left(T_{0}\right)\geq 10q_{\infty}
, ensures
\eta^{(R)}_{K}\equiv 0
on
\supp{U_{L}}
and vice versa for small enough
\varepsilon>0
.

We look for a solution to the Euler equations

E_{1}[\Psi,\omega](x,t)=E_{2}[\Psi,\omega](x,t)=0\quad(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[T_{0},T]

(2.30)

(see (
2.26
) for the definition of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
) of the form

\Psi(x,t)=\Psi_{*}(x,t)+\psi_{*}(x,t),\quad\omega(x,t)=\omega_{*}(x,t)+\phi_{*}(x,t),

where
\psi_{*}
and
\phi_{*}
are small corrections of the previously found first approximations. We decompose these reminders as follows

\psi_{*}=\eta^{(R)}_{K}\psi_{*R}-\eta^{(L)}_{K}\psi_{*L}+\psi^{out}_{*},\quad\phi_{*}=\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\phi_{*R}-\phi_{*L}\right).

In terms of

\psi_{*}^{in}=(\psi_{*R},\psi_{*L}),\quad\psi^{out}_{*},\quad\phi_{*}^{in}=(\phi_{*R},\phi_{*L})

the Euler equations become

\displaystyle E_{1}\left[\Psi,\omega\right]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-4}\left(E_{*R1}\left(\phi_{*R},\psi_{*R},\psi_{*}^{out};\xi\right)-E_{*L1}\left(\phi_{*L},\psi_{*L},\psi_{*}^{out};\xi\right)\right)=0,

\displaystyle\ E_{2}\left[\Psi,\omega\right]

\displaystyle=E_{*2}^{out}\left(\psi_{*}^{in},\psi_{*}^{out};\xi\right)=0,

where, recalling (
2.28
),

\displaystyle E_{*R1}\left(\phi_{*R},\psi_{*R},\psi_{*}^{out}\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}+\phi_{*R}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Psi_{*}+\psi_{*R}+\psi_{*}^{out}\right)\cdot\nabla\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}+\phi_{*R}\right),

\displaystyle E_{*L1}\left(\phi_{*L},\psi_{*L},\psi_{*}^{out}\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\left(U_{L}+\phi_{L}+\phi_{*L}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Psi_{*}+\psi_{*R}+\psi_{*}^{out}\right)\cdot\nabla\left(U_{L}+\phi_{L}+\phi_{*L}\right),

\displaystyle E_{*2}^{out}\left(\psi_{*}^{out},\phi_{*}^{in},\psi_{*}^{in}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta\psi_{*}^{out}+\left(\psi_{*R}\Delta\eta^{(R)}_{K}+2\nabla\eta^{(R)}_{K}\cdot\nabla\psi_{*R}\right)-\left(\psi_{*L}\Delta\eta^{(L)}_{K}+2\nabla\eta^{(L)}_{K}\cdot\nabla\psi_{*L}\right)

\displaystyle+E_{2}[\Psi_{*},\omega_{*}].

The
inner-outer gluing method consists of finding
\psi_{*}^{in}
,
\psi_{*}^{out}
,
\phi_{*}^{in}
to satisfy the following system of coupled equations

\displaystyle E_{*j1}\left(\phi_{*j},\psi_{*j},\psi_{*}^{out}\right)

\displaystyle=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[T_{0},T],\quad\phi_{*j}(\cdot,T)=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}

(2.31)

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi_{*j}

\displaystyle=\phi_{*j}\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[T_{0},T]

for
j=R,\,L
and

E_{*2}^{out}\left(\psi_{*}^{out},\phi_{*}^{in},\psi_{*}^{in}\right)=0\quad{\mbox{in}}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\times[T_{0},T],\quad\psi_{*}^{out}(x_{1},0,t)=0\quad{\mbox{for all }}t\in[T_{0},T].

(2.32)

A solution of the system (
2.31
)-(
2.32
), denoted as
(\phi_{*}^{in},\psi_{*}^{in},\psi_{*}^{out})
, can be used to construct a solution for equation (
2.30
) by a straightforward addition. If we ensure that the remainders
\psi_{*}
and
\phi_{*}
are suitably small, which can be achieved by adjusting
\tilde{\xi}
in (
2.20
) suitably, we can obtain the desired solution. To do this, we will formulate the system as a fixed-point problem for a compact operator within a ball of an appropriate Banach space. We will then find a solution using a degree-theoretical argument, which entails establishing a priori estimates for a homotopical deformation of the problem into a linear one.

It should be noted that in order to construct a solution on
\left[T_{0},T\right]
we require good a priori estimates on a certain linearized operator. For this we need a lower bound on a quadratic form that formally satisfies the structure

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi^{2}}{\mathfrak{K}}-\Phi\left(-\Delta\right)^{-1}\left(\Phi\right)\right),

(2.33)

for some fixed profile
\mathfrak{K}
and some
\Phi
. Bounds of this nature are also important in
[
10
,
14
,
15
]
. Crucially, in those works,
\mathfrak{K}
is positive on all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, so the integral makes sense with some decay assumptions on
\Phi
. However,
\mathfrak{K}
in our case would correspond to a certain function with compact support related to the vorticity of the vortex pair introduced in Section
2.1
, and there would be no guarantee that the support of
\Phi
matches the support of
\mathfrak{K}
in general, rendering (
2.33
) a meaningless quantity. Thus a large part of Section
5
, and indeed one of the most subtle parts of our construction, is devoted to obtaining lower bounds on a quantity that has similar structure to (
2.33
), and using these bounds to obtain a priori estimates on the aforementioned linearized operator, which allows us to construct our solution on
\left[T_{0},T\right]
.

The above construction gives us a sequence of solutions indexed by
T
, for arbitrarily large
T
, satisfying (
2.31
), and good time decay estimates on
\left[T_{0},T\right]
. To construct a solution as stated in Theorem
1.1
, we then use this time decay to obtain equicontinuity for this sequence of solutions on
\left[T_{0},T\right]
, with
0
terminal condition at
T
, which along with uniform boundedness, allows to extract a subsequence, whose uniform limit solves (
1.1
) on
\left[T_{0},\infty\right)
, as desired.

We can now also better describe
\xi_{re}=\left(p_{re},q_{re}\right)
defined in Theorem
1.1
. They take the form

\displaystyle p_{re}

\displaystyle=\bar{p}+\hat{p},

\displaystyle q_{re}

\displaystyle=\bar{q}+\hat{q},

where
\left(\bar{p},\bar{q}\right)
is defined in (
2.17
), and
\left(\hat{p},\hat{q}\right)
defined in (
2.20
). They satisfy bounds

\displaystyle\|t^{-1}\bar{p}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}+\|\dot{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

(2.34)

\displaystyle\|t^{2}\bar{q}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}+\|t^{3}\dot{\bar{q}}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\|t{\hat{p}}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}+\|t^{2}\dot{\hat{p}}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}\leq C\varepsilon^{3-\sigma}

\displaystyle\|t^{2}{\hat{q}}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}+\|t^{3}\dot{\hat{q}}\|_{L^{\infty}[T_{0},\infty]}\leq C\varepsilon^{3-\sigma}.

The remainder of this paper will be dedicated to meticulously carrying out the steps mentioned above.

3
Point Vortex Trajectories for Two Vortex Pairs

Here we construct the main order point vortex dynamics
\xi_{0}(t)=\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)
introduced in (
2.17
) that will be exhibited by the solution to our full dynamic problem modelling two vortex pairs moving away from each other with with equal speed and opposite direction.

We start by solving the ODE system (
2.13
). A previous study was carried out by Saffman
[
28
]
. It is convenient to make the change of variables

\displaystyle p_{*{}}+iq_{*{}}=r(t)e^{i\vartheta(t)},

(3.1)

the system (
2.13
) becomes

\frac{\dot{r}}{r}=-2\cot{\left(2\vartheta\right)}\dot{\vartheta},\quad\dot{\vartheta}=-\frac{m}{2r^{2}}.

(3.2)

We wish to solve this on
[0,\infty)
with data given by

\lim_{t\to\infty}q_{*{}}(t)=q_{\infty}>0,\quad\vartheta(0)=\vartheta_{0}\in\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right),

where
q_{\infty}
agrees with our choice in (
2.15
). Then our solution to (
3.2
) is given by

\vartheta(t)=\frac{1}{2}\arccot{\left(\cot{2\vartheta_{0}}+\frac{mt}{C^{2}}\right)},\quad r(t)=\frac{C}{\sin{2\vartheta(t)}},

where
C
is a constant of integration. This means that our solution to (
2.13
) is given by

p_{*{}}(t)=\frac{C}{2\sin{\vartheta(t)}},\quad q_{*{}}(t)=\frac{C}{2\cos{\vartheta(t)}},

and thus we can see that
C=2q_{\infty}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\vartheta(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\arccot{\left(\cot{2\vartheta_{0}}+\frac{mt}{4q_{\infty}^{2}}\right)},\quad p_{*{}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{\infty}}{\sin{\vartheta(t)}},\quad q_{*{}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{\infty}}{\cos{\vartheta(t)}}.

(3.3)

One can check directly that as
t\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\vartheta(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}{\left(\frac{1}{1+t}\right)},\quad p_{*{}}(t)

\displaystyle=P_{0}+\frac{mt}{2q_{\infty}}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\frac{1}{t+1}\right)},\quad q_{*{}}(t)

\displaystyle=q_{\infty}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\frac{1}{\left(t+1\right)^{2}}\right)},

(3.4)

where

P_{0}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(p_{*{}}-\frac{mt}{2q_{\infty}}\right).

From these asymptotics and the system (
2.13
), we can deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|(t+1)^{-1}p_{*{}}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\dot{p}_{*{}}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1},

\displaystyle\left\|q_{*{}}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|(t+1)^{3}\dot{q}_{*{}}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1},

where
C_{1}>0
is an absolute constant and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\infty}
is the
L^{\infty}
norm on
[0,\infty)
.

For a generic
\varepsilon>0
and
t
, the vortex trajectories

\xi_{0}(t)=\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)
in (
2.17
)
obey the modified system (
2.16
) that we recall here

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2}\left(\frac{1}{q_{0}}-\frac{q_{0}}{p_{0}^{2}+q_{0}^{2}}\right)+g\left(q_{0}^{\prime}\right)=\mathcal{F}\left(q_{0},p_{0}\right)+g\left(q_{0}^{\prime}\right),

\displaystyle\dot{q}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{m}{2}\left(\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}^{2}+q_{0}^{2}}\right)=-\mathcal{F}\left(p_{0},q_{0}\right)

where
g
is the
\varepsilon^{2}
-correction to the speed of an idealized vortex pair, as described in Theorem
2.1
and (
2.10
).
We construct the solution to the system (
2.16
) in the form

\xi_{0}(t)=\xi_{*{}}(t)+\overline{\xi}(t),\quad\overline{\xi}(t)=\left(\overline{p}(t),\overline{q}(t)\right),

where
\overline{\xi}
will be a small error term. To do this we need the linearisation of (
2.13
) around the solution (
3.3
). It can be directly checked that this linearisation is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\coloneqq\frac{d}{dt}+\mathscr{A}\left(\xi_{*{}}\right),

(3.5)

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}\left(\xi_{*{}}\right)=\frac{m}{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{2p_{*{}}q_{*{}}}{\left(p_{*{}}^{2}+q_{*{}}^{2}\right)^{2}}&\frac{1}{q_{*{}}^{2}}+\frac{\left(p_{*{}}^{2}-q_{*{}}^{2}\right)}{\left(p_{*{}}^{2}+q_{*{}}^{2}\right)^{2}}\\
-\frac{1}{p_{*{}}^{2}}+\frac{\left(p_{*{}}^{2}-q_{*{}}^{2}\right)}{\left(p_{*{}}^{2}+q_{*{}}^{2}\right)^{2}}&\frac{2p_{*{}}q_{*{}}}{\left(p_{*{}}^{2}+q_{*{}}^{2}\right)^{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.6)

Converting to polar coordinates as in (
3.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}\left(\xi_{*{}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{m}{2r^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sin{2\vartheta}&-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}{\vartheta}}-\cos{2\vartheta}\\
\frac{1}{\cos^{2}{\vartheta}}-\cos{2\vartheta}&-\sin{2\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\dot{\vartheta}\begin{pmatrix}\sin{2\vartheta}&-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}{\vartheta}}-\cos{2\vartheta}\\
\frac{1}{\cos^{2}{\vartheta}}-\cos{2\vartheta}&-\sin{2\vartheta}\end{pmatrix},

(3.7)

where the second equality in (
3.7
) comes from (
3.2
). Thus we see that
\mathscr{A}=\dot{\mathscr{B}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}\left(\xi_{*{}}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\cos{2\vartheta}&\frac{\cos{\vartheta}}{\sin{\vartheta}}-\frac{1}{2}\sin{2\vartheta}\\
\frac{\sin{\vartheta}}{\cos{\vartheta}}-\frac{1}{2}\sin{2\vartheta}&\frac{1}{2}\cos{2\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.8)

Next, to calculate the exponential of
\pm\mathscr{B}
, we note that

\left|\mathscr{B}-\lambda\mathbb{I}\right|=\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{4},

so diagonalising
\mathscr{B}
, taking the exponential, and then calculating the result leaves us with

e^{\mathscr{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{1}{2}}\sin^{2}{\vartheta}+e^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cos^{2}{\vartheta}&\frac{2\sinh{\frac{1}{2}}\cos^{3}{\vartheta}}{\sin{\vartheta}}\\
\frac{2\sinh{\frac{1}{2}}\sin^{3}{\vartheta}}{\cos{\vartheta}}&e^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sin^{2}{\vartheta}+e^{\frac{1}{2}}\cos^{2}{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix},

and

\displaystyle e^{-\mathscr{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sin^{2}{\vartheta}+e^{\frac{1}{2}}\cos^{2}{\vartheta}&-\frac{2\sinh{\frac{1}{2}}\cos^{3}{\vartheta}}{\sin{\vartheta}}\\
-\frac{2\sinh{\frac{1}{2}}\sin^{3}{\vartheta}}{\cos{\vartheta}}&e^{\frac{1}{2}}\sin^{2}{\vartheta}+e^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cos^{2}{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.9)

From (
3.3
), we obtain that as
t\to\infty
,

\displaystyle e^{\pm\mathscr{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}{\left(1\right)}&\mathcal{O}{\left(t+1\right)}\\
\mathcal{O}{\left(\left(t+1\right)^{-3}\right)}&\mathcal{O}{\left(1\right)}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.10)

From (
3.5
)–(
3.9
), can write our linearised operator
\mathscr{L}
as

\mathscr{L}=e^{-\mathscr{B}}\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{\mathscr{B}}\cdot\right).

Then finding a solution to (
2.16
) of the form
\xi_{0}=\xi_{*{}}+\overline{\xi}
is formally equivalent to the problem

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\overline{\xi}=\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{M}_{g}\overline{\xi}+\mathscr{N}\left(\overline{\xi}\right),

(3.11)

where

\mathscr{R}=\begin{pmatrix}g\left(q_{*{}}^{\prime}\right)\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathscr{M}_{g}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\partial_{q^{\prime}}g\left(q_{*{}}^{\prime}\right)}{\varepsilon}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

and
\mathscr{N}\left(\overline{\xi}\right)
are the remaining terms which are quadratic or higher in
\overline{\xi}
given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}\left(\overline{\xi}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{q_{*{}}^{\prime}}^{q_{*{}}^{\prime}+\overline{q}^{\prime}}\partial^{2}_{Q}g(Q)\left(q_{*{}}^{\prime}+\overline{q}^{\prime}-Q\right)dQ+\mathcal{F}\left(q_{*{}}+\overline{q},p_{*{}}+\overline{p}\right)-\mathcal{F}\left(q_{*{}},p_{*{}}\right)+\left(\mathscr{A}\overline{\xi}\right)_{1},

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}\left(\overline{\xi}\right)_{2}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{F}\left(p_{*{}}+\overline{p},q_{*{}}+\overline{q}\right)+\mathcal{F}\left(p_{*{}},q_{*{}}\right)+\left(\mathscr{A}\overline{\xi}\right)_{2}.

To formulate (
3.11
) as an integral equation for which we can used fixed point machinery, we define the operator
\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}
by

\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}\xi=\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}=e^{-\mathscr{B}}\begin{pmatrix}\int_{0}^{t}\left(e^{\mathscr{B}}\xi\right)_{1}\\
\int_{t}^{\infty}\left(e^{\mathscr{B}}\xi\right)_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Next, we define
\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}
to be the space of functions
\xi=\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right):[0,\infty)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
\xi_{1}(0)=0
and

\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}}\coloneqq\left\|\left(t+1\right)^{-1}\xi_{1}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\left(t+1\right)^{2}\xi_{2}\right\|_{\infty}<\infty.

Now, let
B
be the ball radius
\varepsilon^{2}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|
in
\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}
:

\displaystyle B=\left\{\xi\in\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}:\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\right\}.

(3.12)

Finally we define the operator
\mathscr{T}
on
B
by

\mathscr{T}\xi\coloneqq\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}\left(\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{M}_{g}\xi+\mathscr{N}\left(\xi\right)\right).

Given these definitions, we are ready to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

For
\varepsilon>0
small enough, there is a unique solution
\xi_{0}=\xi_{*{}}+\overline{\xi}
to (
2.16
) with
\overline{p}(0)=0
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\overline{q}(t)=0
in the ball
B
defined in (
3.12
). This solution has the bound

\displaystyle\left\|(t+1)^{-1}\overline{p}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\dot{\overline{p}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\left\|(t+1)^{2}\overline{q}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\left(t+1\right)^{3}\dot{\overline{q}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

(3.14)

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Proof.

We first note that from (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we have

\left|g\right|+\left|\partial_{q^{\prime}}g\right|+\left|\partial_{q^{\prime}}^{2}g\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

Then, given the definitions of
\mathscr{R}
,
\mathscr{M}_{g}
, and
\mathscr{N}
, we have that for any
\xi\in B
,

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{M}_{g}\xi+\mathscr{N}\left(\xi\right)\right)_{1}\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{M}_{g}\xi+\mathscr{N}\left(\xi\right)\right)_{2}\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{4}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}}{\left(t+1\right)^{3}}.

From the asymptotics of
e^{\pm\mathscr{B}}
given in (
3.10
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}\left(\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{M}_{g}\xi+\mathscr{N}\left(\xi\right)\right)\right)_{1}\right|\leq C(t+1)\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}\left(\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{M}_{g}\xi+\mathscr{N}\left(\xi\right)\right)\right)_{2}\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{\left(t+1\right)^{2}},

meaning that for
\xi\in B
,

\left\|\mathscr{T}\xi\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

some absolute constant
C
. Thus for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, we have
\mathscr{T}:B\to B
. Moreover, since

\left\|\mathscr{T}\xi_{1}-\mathscr{T}\xi_{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}}\leq C\varepsilon\left\|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}},

we have that
\mathscr{T}:B\to B
is a contraction for small enough
\varepsilon>0
, and thus the fixed point problem

\mathscr{T}\xi=\xi

has a unique solution in
B
, which we call
\overline{\xi}
. Then by construction,
\xi_{0}=\xi_{*{}}+\overline{\xi}
solves (
2.16
) with
\overline{p}(0)=0
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\overline{q}=0
, and
\overline{\xi}
has the bound

\left\|\overline{\xi}\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}}=\left\|(t+1)^{-1}\overline{p}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|(t+1)^{2}\overline{q}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\left\|\mathscr{K}_{\left(0,\infty\right)}\mathscr{R}\right\|_{\mathscr{X}_{\left(-1,2\right)}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

and the bounds for
\dot{\overline{p}}
and
\dot{\overline{q}}
claimed in (
3.13
)–(
3.14
) can be checked directly.
∎

4
First Approximation on
\left[T_{0},T\right]

Our first step in building a two vortex pair solution to the Euler equations (
1.1
) is to construct a good enough first approximation on a finite time interval
\left[T_{0},T\right]
defined in (
4.19
)–(
4.20
). As discussed in Section
2
,
T_{0}
will be some absolute constant large enough to ensure sufficient separation of the two vortex pairs, and
T
should be thought as an arbitrarily large parameter such that we will take
T\to\infty
to construct our full solution on
\left[T_{0},\infty\right)
. To start, we wish to calculate the size of

\displaystyle E_{1}\left[\omega_{*},\Psi_{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\omega_{*}(x,t)+\nabla^{\perp}\Psi_{*}(x,t)\cdot\nabla\omega_{*}(x,t),

(4.1)

\displaystyle E_{2}\left[\omega_{*},\Psi_{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta\Psi_{*}(x,t)+\omega_{*}(x,t).

(4.2)

for well chosen
\omega_{*}
and
\Psi_{*}
. First, let

\displaystyle\xi(t)=(p(t),q(t)),

be a time dependent vector field, and let
c(t)
be dependent on
q(t)
as in (
2.23
) so that

\displaystyle c(t)=\frac{m}{2q(t)}+g\left(\frac{q(t)}{\varepsilon}\right),

(4.3)

and
\Omega(t)
as in (
2.8
) so that

\displaystyle\Omega(t)=m+\left(\frac{m\log{2q(t)}-c(t)q(t)}{\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|}\right).

(4.4)

We will restrict ourselves to
\xi(t)
of the form

\displaystyle\xi(t)=\xi_{0}(t)+\xi_{1}(t)+\tilde{\xi}(t),

(4.5)

where
\xi_{0}(t)=\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)
,
\xi_{1}(t)=\left(p_{1}(t),q_{1}(t)\right)
, and
\tilde{\xi}(t)=\left(\tilde{p}(t),\tilde{q}(t)\right)
.

Here
\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)
was constructed in Section
3
as a small perturbation of
\left(p_{*{}}(t),q_{*{}}(t)\right)
, the solution to the point vortex ODE system obtained from the configuration of two vortex pairs we are considering, given in (
2.13
).

Next,
\left(p_{1}(t),q_{1}(t)\right)
will be a small perturbation of
\left(p_{0}(t),q_{0}(t)\right)
obtained analogously to the construction of
\left(\bar{p},\bar{q}\right)
in Section
3
. It will correspond to an adjustment of the centres of each vortex so that the first approximation we build has small enough error to carry out a bootstrap argument in Section
5
.

Finally,
\left(\tilde{p}(t),\tilde{q}(t)\right)
will be a parameter vector field used to adjust the centres of the vortices once again to obtain a genuine solution to (
1.1
) on
\left[T_{0},T\right]
. From Section
3
, we know that
\left(p_{0},q_{0}\right)
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|(t+1)^{-1}p_{0}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\dot{p}_{0}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\left\|q_{0}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|(t+1)^{3}\dot{q}_{0}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1},

(4.7)

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. In this section we will construct a first approximation with
\left(p_{1},q_{1}\right)
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|tp_{1}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},\infty\right)}+\left\|t^{2}\dot{p}_{1}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},\infty\right)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\left\|t^{2}q_{1}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},\infty\right)}+\left\|t^{3}\dot{q}_{1}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},\infty\right)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|,

(4.9)

for some constant
C_{2}>0
, with the parameter
\tilde{\xi}
restricted to the ball

\displaystyle\left\|t\tilde{p}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},T\right]}+\left\|t^{2}\dot{\tilde{p}}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},T\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{4-\sigma},\ \ \tilde{p}\left(T\right)=0,

(4.10)

\displaystyle\left\|t^{2}\tilde{q}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},T\right]}+\left\|t^{3}\dot{\tilde{q}}\right\|_{\left[T_{0},T\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{4-\sigma},\ \ \tilde{q}\left(T\right)=0,

(4.11)

for fixed, arbitrarily small
\sigma>0
, where the
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\left[T_{0},T\right]}
norm used is the
L^{\infty}
norm for functions from
\left[T_{0},T\right]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and analogously for
\left[T_{0},\infty\right)
. Recall that
q_{*{}}\to q_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
.

From the bounds on
\left(p,q\right)
in (
4.6
)–(
4.11
), we also have the following estimates on the linearisation of certain functions of
\xi
around
\xi_{0}
that we will use repeatedly.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\xi=\left(p,q\right)
and
\xi_{0}=\left(p_{0},q_{0}\right)
be as in (
4.5
). Then on
\left[T_{0},T\right]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{0}^{n}}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|}{t^{3}}\right)},\ \ n\in\left\{2,3,4\right\},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q_{0}^{n}}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|}{t^{2}}\right)},\ \ n\in\left\{2,3,4\right\},

and

\displaystyle\frac{p^{i}q^{j}}{\left(p^{2}+q^{2}\right)^{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{0}^{i}q_{0}^{j}}{\left(p_{0}^{2}+q_{0}^{2}\right)^{k}}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|}{t^{3}}\right)},\ \ k\in\left\{2,3,4\right\},\ \ i,j\in\left\{0,1,2,3,4\right\},\ \ i+j\leq k.

Recalling the definition of
\Gamma
from (
1.6
),
\psi_{\frac{q}{\varepsilon}}
from (
2.5
) with the definition of
\xi
(
4.5
) in hand, and
c(t),\Omega(t)
related to
q(t)
by (
4.3
)–(
4.4
), we define as in (
2.25
),

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{R}(x,t)-\Psi_{L}(x,t),

(4.12)

\displaystyle\omega_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}\left(U_{R}(x,t)-U_{L}(x,t)\right),

with

\displaystyle\Psi_{R}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}-qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\Gamma\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}+qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)+\psi_{\frac{q}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\Psi_{L}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}-qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\Gamma\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}+qe_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)+\psi_{\frac{q}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right),

and

\displaystyle U_{R}(x,t)

\displaystyle=f_{\varepsilon}\left(\Psi_{R}-cx_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{R}(x)-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(qe_{2}\right)}\left(x-pe_{1}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(-\Psi_{R}(x)+cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(-qe_{2}\right)}\left(x-pe_{1}\right),

(4.14)

\displaystyle U_{L}(x,t)

\displaystyle=f_{\varepsilon}\left(\Psi_{L}+cx_{2}\right),

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{L}(x)+cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(qe_{2}\right)}\left(x+pe_{1}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(-\Psi_{L}(x)-cx_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma}_{+}\chi_{B_{s}\left(-qe_{2}\right)}\left(x+pe_{1}\right)

with
f_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
2.6
). We have

-\Delta\Psi_{j}=\varepsilon^{-2}U_{j},\ \ \ j=L,R.

Next, define cutoffs by

\displaystyle\eta^{(R)}_{K}(x,t)\coloneqq\eta_{0}\left(\frac{\left|x-pe_{1}\right|}{Kt}\right),

(4.15)

\displaystyle\eta^{(L)}_{K}(x,t)\coloneqq\eta_{0}\left(\frac{\left|x+pe_{1}\right|}{Kt}\right).

(4.16)

where
\eta_{0}(r)
is a cutoff that is
1
on
r\leq 1
and
0
on
r\geq 2
. Recall that due to Remark
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\supp U_{R}\subset B_{\varepsilon\rho}\left(p,q\right)\cup B_{\varepsilon\rho}\left(p,-q\right),

(4.17)

for some absolute constant
\rho>0
, even with time dependence, due to the sizes of
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},
and
\tilde{\xi}
stated in (
4.6
)–(
4.11
). We also recall the choice of
K
in Section
2.4
given by

\displaystyle K=5q_{\infty},

(4.18)

and for the rest of the main body of the paper until Section
6
, we also consider the full dynamic problem (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) from times
t\geq T_{0}
, where, as in Theorem
1.1
,
T_{0}
is some absolute constant large enough to ensure

\displaystyle p_{*}\left(T_{0}\right)\geq 10q_{\infty}.

(4.19)

Then, due to (
3.4
), (
4.6
)–(
4.11
), for small enough
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle p\left(T_{0}\right)\geq 9q_{\infty}.

The choice of
K
in (
4.18
) ensures
\eta^{(j)}_{K}\equiv 1
on
\supp{U_{j}}
for
j=R,L
, and the choice of
T_{0}
ensures
\eta^{(R)}_{K}\equiv 0
on
\supp{U_{L}}
and vice versa for small enough
\varepsilon>0
. As per the strategy laid out in Section
2
, we will first construct a sequence of solutions on time intervals
\left[T_{0},T\right]
for each
T>0
large enough, from which we will extract a solution to (
1.1
) on
\left[T_{0},\infty\right)
. Accordingly, following (
2.18
), we also fix
T>0
, a large time satisfying

\displaystyle T\gg T_{0}.

(4.20)

Remark 4.2
.

We remark here that as long we define
T_{0}
in a manner independent of
\varepsilon
, we can enlarge it as we like as long as it satisfies (
4.19
). Indeed, we will do so in order to close a crucial a priori estimate in Lemma
5.9
.

Now we define
\psi_{R},\psi_{L}
and
\phi_{R},\phi_{L}
of the form

\displaystyle\psi_{R}

\displaystyle=\psi_{R}\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}}{\varepsilon},t\right),\quad\psi_{L}=\psi_{L}\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}}{\varepsilon},t\right),

\displaystyle\phi_{R}

\displaystyle=\phi_{R}\left(\frac{x-pe_{1}}{\varepsilon},t\right),\quad\phi_{L}=\phi_{L}\left(\frac{x+pe_{1}}{\varepsilon},t\right)

such that for
j=R,L
,

\displaystyle\Delta\psi_{j}+\phi_{j}=0

(4.21)

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with
\psi_{j}
in
L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
and odd in
x_{2}
(equivalently
\psi_{j}
in
L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\right)
and
\psi_{R}\left(\frac{x_{1}-p}{\varepsilon},0,t\right)=0
, and analogously for
\psi_{L}
).

We set

\psi^{in}=\left(\psi_{R},\psi_{L}\right),\quad\phi^{in}=\left(\phi_{R},\phi_{L}\right).

(4.22)

Given these definitions, we then set

\displaystyle\Psi_{*}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{0}+\eta^{(R)}_{K}\psi_{R}-\eta^{(L)}_{K}\psi_{L}+\psi^{out},

(4.23)

\displaystyle\omega_{*}

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}+\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\phi_{R}-\phi_{L}\right),

(4.24)

for some
\psi^{out}=\psi^{out}(x,t)
. From (
4.23
)–(
4.24
), we can see that

\displaystyle E_{1}\left[\omega_{*},\Psi_{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-4}\left(E_{R1}\left(\phi_{R},\psi_{R},\psi^{out};\xi\right)-E_{L1}\left(\phi_{L},\psi_{L},\psi^{out};\xi\right)\right),

(4.25)

and

E_{2}\left[\omega_{*},\Psi_{*}\right]=E_{2}^{out}\left(\psi^{in},\psi^{out};\xi\right),

where

\displaystyle E_{R1}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Psi_{0}+\psi_{R}+\psi^{out}\right)\cdot\nabla\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right),

(4.26)

\displaystyle E_{L1}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\left(U_{L}+\phi_{L}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla^{\perp}\left(\Psi_{0}-\psi_{L}+\psi^{out}\right)\cdot\nabla\left(U_{L}+\phi_{L}\right),

(4.27)

\displaystyle E_{2}^{out}=\Delta\psi^{out}+\left(\psi_{R}\Delta\eta^{(R)}_{K}+2\nabla\eta^{(R)}_{K}\cdot\nabla\psi_{R}\right)-\left(\psi_{L}\Delta\eta^{(L)}_{K}+2\nabla\eta^{(L)}_{K}\cdot\nabla\psi_{L}\right).

(4.28)

We note that the specific forms of
E_{R1}
,
E_{L1}
, and
E_{2}^{out}
are due to the fact that
\phi_{R}
and
\phi_{L}
will have the same supports as
U_{R}
and
U_{L}
respectively, and so due to the choice of
K
and
T_{0}
in (
4.18
)–(
4.19
),
\eta^{(j)}_{K}\equiv 1
on
\supp{\phi_{j}}
for
j=R,L
, and
\eta^{(R)}_{K}\equiv 0
on
\supp{\phi_{L}}
and vice versa.

Let

\displaystyle y=\frac{x-pe_{1}}{\varepsilon},\ q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{\varepsilon},\ p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{\varepsilon}.

(4.29)

We first prove a lemma that shows both
E_{R1}
and
E_{L1}
can be written in ways that will be advantageous to us later.

Lemma 4.3
.

With change of coordinates given by (
4.29
), we can write
E_{R1}
, defined in (
4.26
), as

\displaystyle E_{R1}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}-\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(\Delta\psi_{R}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\psi_{R}\right)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)\left(y_{2}-\sgn{\left(y_{2}\right)}q^{\prime}\right)-\varepsilon\dot{q}\sgn{\left(y_{2}\right)}y_{1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi^{out}\cdot\grad_{y}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right)-\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)e_{1}\right]\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}+\dot{q}\left(\varepsilon\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\mathcal{R}_{1}+\varepsilon\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-}\mathcal{R}_{2}\right),

(4.30)

where
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is a remainder term,
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is its odd reflection in
y_{2}
, and for small enough
\varepsilon>0
, on the support of
U_{R}
restricted to the upper half plane,

\displaystyle\varepsilon\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{q^{\prime}}g\left(q^{\prime}\right)\left(\frac{\varrho_{1}(\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|)}{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|}+1\right)\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)-\frac{m\varepsilon}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}\left(\frac{\varrho_{2}(\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|)}{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}+1\right)\left(y_{1}^{2}-\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon^{5}\right)},

(4.31)

where
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
are radial functions in
y-q^{\prime}e_{2}
defined in (
A.3
). An analogous statement holds for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
in coordinates
y+q^{\prime}e_{2}
on the support of
U_{R}
restricted to the lower plane. An analogous statement also holds for
E_{L1}
in the appropriate coordinates on the support of
U_{L}
.

Proof.

Using the change of coordinates (
4.29
), we have

\displaystyle E_{R1}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{R}+\varepsilon\left(-\left(\dot{p}-c+c\right)e_{1}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right)+\varepsilon\dot{q}\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-\Psi_{L}+\psi_{R}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right)+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi^{out}\cdot\grad_{y}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right).

Note we have written
\dot{p}
as
\dot{p}-c+c
in the second term above. This is to take advantage of the fact that

\displaystyle-\varepsilon ce_{1}\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}=0.

(4.32)

Indeed, (
4.32
) then allows us to obtain

\displaystyle E_{R1}=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{R}-\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)e_{1}\right]\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi^{out}\cdot\grad_{y}\left(U_{R}+\phi_{R}\right)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left(\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}\right]-\varepsilon ce_{1}\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}-\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)e_{1}\right]\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}+\varepsilon\dot{q}\partial_{q^{\prime}}U_{R}\right)}_{\mathcal{A}}.

(4.33)

The first line on the right hand side of (
4.33
) are all terms on the right hand side of (
4.30
). It remains to show that the remaining terms on the right hand side of (
4.33
), labelled
\mathcal{A}
, can be written as the remaining terms on the right hand side of (
4.30
). Note that

\displaystyle\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}\right]-\varepsilon ce_{1}\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}+\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\psi_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}-\nabla^{\perp}_{y}U_{R}\cdot\grad_{y}\psi_{R}.

We know
U_{R}=f_{\varepsilon}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)
, and (
4.21
) gives
-\Delta\psi_{R}=\phi_{R}
, thus we get

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\phi_{R}-\nabla^{\perp}_{y}U_{R}\grad_{y}\psi_{R}\cdot=-\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(\Delta\psi_{R}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\psi_{R}\right).

Next we note that

\displaystyle-\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)e_{1}\right]\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}=\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)y_{2}\right)\right).

However, if we define

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma\left[\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma-1}_{+}\chi_{B_{\frac{s}{\varepsilon}}\left(q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)}(y)+\left(-\Psi_{R}+c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)^{\gamma-1}_{+}\chi_{B_{\frac{s}{\varepsilon}}\left(-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)}(y)\right]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}+\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-},

with
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{\pm}
being the terms supported above and below the
y_{1}
axis respectively, we can see that due to (
4.17
), having
\supp{\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}}\subset B_{\rho}\left(q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)\subset\left\{y_{2}>0\right\}
,
\supp{\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-}}\subset B_{\rho}\left(-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)\subset\left\{y_{2}<0\right\}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle-\left[\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)e_{1}\right]\cdot\grad_{y}U_{R}=\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(\Psi_{L}+\varepsilon\left(\dot{p}-c\right)\left(y_{2}-\sgn{\left(y_{2}\right)}q^{\prime}\right)\right)\right).

Now we concentrate on
\varepsilon\dot{q}\partial_{q^{\prime}}U_{R}
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon\dot{q}\partial_{q^{\prime}}U_{R}=\varepsilon\dot{q}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)-\varepsilon\dot{q}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-}\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(-\Psi_{R}+c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right).

Note that due to the choice of
\Omega
, (
2.7
), on the support of
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}
, we can write

\displaystyle\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)-\Gamma\left(y+q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\psi_{q^{\prime}}(y,t)-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\left(\Gamma\left(2q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)-\Gamma\left(y+q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)\right)-c\varepsilon\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)+\psi_{q^{\prime}}\left(y,t\right)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\frac{m}{2}\log{\left(1+\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}\right)}-c\varepsilon\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)+\psi_{q^{\prime}}(y,t).

(4.34)

Thus we can see that, on the support of
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{2}\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)-\frac{m}{2}\frac{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{2\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}}{1+\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}}+c\varepsilon-\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\frac{m}{2}\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(\log{\left(1+\frac{z_{2}}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{\left|z\right|^{2}}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}\right)}\right)\bigg\rvert_{z=y-q^{\prime}e_{2}}-\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(c\right)\varepsilon\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right).

(4.35)

By inspection, the first line on the right hand side of (
4.35
) is equal to

\displaystyle-\partial_{2}\left(\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)-\Gamma\left(y+q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\psi_{q^{\prime}}(y,t)-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right),

and so on the support of
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}
, we can write (
4.35
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)=-\partial_{2}\left(\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)-\Gamma\left(y+q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\psi_{q^{\prime}}(y,t)-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)+\mathcal{R}_{1}(y,t),

where
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is the second line on the right hand side of (
4.35
).

Similarly, on the support of
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle-\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(-\Psi_{R}+c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)=\partial_{2}\left(\Gamma\left(y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)-\Gamma\left(y+q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\psi_{q^{\prime}}(y,t)-c\varepsilon y_{2}-\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\Omega\right)+\mathcal{R}_{2}(y,t),

where
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is the odd reflection of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
in
y_{2}
. Thus

\displaystyle\varepsilon\dot{q}\partial_{q^{\prime}}U_{R}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\dot{q}f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sgn{\left(y_{2}\right)}e_{2}\cdot\grad_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)+\varepsilon\dot{q}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\mathcal{R}_{1}+\varepsilon\dot{q}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-}\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}_{y}\left(\Psi_{R}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\cdot\grad_{y}\left(\varepsilon\dot{q}f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sgn{\left(y_{2}\right)}y_{1}\right)+\varepsilon\dot{q}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\mathcal{R}_{1}+\varepsilon\dot{q}\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{-}\mathcal{R}_{2}.

It remains to show that
\varepsilon\mathcal{R}_{1}
can be written in the form (
4.31
). The analogous statement for
\varepsilon\mathcal{R}_{2}
will follow by symmetry. We first have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}=-\frac{m}{2}\frac{\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}{2\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}}{1+\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}}-\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(c\right)\varepsilon\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)+\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right).

(4.36)

On the support of
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}
, the first two terms on the right hand side of (
4.36
) can be written as

\displaystyle-\frac{m}{2\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)\left(1-\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)}\right)-\frac{m}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}\left(1+\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon\right)}\right)-\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(c\right)\varepsilon\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right),

where we have used the fact that
q^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\right)}
. This can then be written as

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{m}{2\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}-\varepsilon\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(c\right)\right)\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)-\frac{m}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}\left(\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}-2\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon^{4}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\partial_{q^{\prime}}g\left(q^{\prime}\right)\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)-\frac{m}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}\left(y_{1}^{2}-\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon^{4}\right)},

(4.37)

where for the first term on the right hand side of the second line in (
4.37
) we use (
4.3
).

Then, on the support of
\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left(-\frac{m}{2}\frac{\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}{2\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}}{1+\frac{y_{2}-q^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{\left|y-q^{\prime}e_{2}\right|^{2}}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{2}}}-\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(c\right)\varepsilon\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{q^{\prime}}g\left(q^{\prime}\right)\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)-\frac{m\varepsilon}{4\left(q^{\prime}\right)^{3}}\left(y_{1}^{2}-\left(y_{2}-q^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}{\left(\varepsilon^{5}\right)}.

(4.38)

To bound
\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right)
, we note that it solves the following equation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right)+\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right)=\mathcal{E},

with boundary condition

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right)\left(y_{1},0\right)=\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\left(y_{1},0\right),

and error term
\mathcal{E}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\left(2\partial_{2}\Gamma\left(y+q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)+\partial_{q^{\prime}}\left(c\right)\varepsilon y_{2}+\left|\log{\varepsilon}\right|\left(\partial_{q^{\prime}}\Omega\right)+c\varepsilon\right).

We write
\partial_{q^{\prime}}\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta\beta_{1}=0,\ \ \beta_{1}\left(y_{1},0\right)=\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\left(y_{1},0\right),

(4.39)

\displaystyle\Delta\beta_{2}+\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\beta_{2}=\mathcal{E}-\left(f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right)^{+}\beta_{1},\ \ \beta_{2}\left(y_{1},0\right)=0.

Using the representation formula on the upper half plane, we can write
\beta_{1}
as

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\left(y_{1},y_{2}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{y_{2}}{\left(z_{1}-y_{1}\right)^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\left(z_{1},0\right)dz_{1}.

(4.40)

From the fact that the travelling vortex pair
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
solves
2.1
in
x
coordinates, we know that
\psi_{q^{\prime}}
solves

\displaystyle\Delta\psi_{q^{\prime}}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\psi_{q^{\prime}}+\left(\Delta\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\left(\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}-c\varepsilon y_{2}\right)\right)+N\left[\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta\psi_{q^{\prime}}+V\psi_{q^{\prime}}+E+N\left[\psi_{q^{\prime}}\right]=0.

(4.41)

on the upper half plane, where
\mathring{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
2.5
), and
N
is the nonlinearity we obtain upon rearranging
2.1
as above. Thus, once again using the representation formula on the upper half plane, we have that

\displaystyle\partial_{2}\psi_{q^{\prime}}\left(z_{1},0\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{v_{2}}{\left(v_{1}-z_{1}\right)^{2}+v_{2}^{2}}\left(V\psi_{q^{\prime}}-E+N[\psi_{q^{\prime}}]\right)dv,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B_{\rho}\left(q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)}\frac{v_{2}}{\left(v_{1}-z_{1}\right)^{2}+v_{2}^{2}}\left(V\psi_{q^{\prime}}-E+N[\psi_{q^{\prime}}]\right)dv,

(4.42)

with the last line on the right hand side of (
4.42
), with
\rho>0
defined in (
4.17
), being due to the supports of
V
,
E
, and
N[\psi_{q^{\prime}}]
.

One can see from (
4.41
) and the ansatz (
2.5
) (see
vortex-pair-paper
for more details), that on
B_{\rho}\left(q^{\prime}e_{2}\right)
,

\displaystyle\left(V\psi_{q^{\prime}}-E+N[\psi_{q^{\prime}}]\right)(v)
